The final numbers come from a second, cache-consistent pass (identical input → identical cached\n   answer; $0). The shift was ≤0.002 AUROC for TVJT, and the verdict was unchanged\n   (`logs/repro/verdict_before.json` vs `results/verdict.json`).\n\n### Per-operator detection (controlled mutants of the 300 screen golds)\n\n| operator | n mutants | TVJT pairwise (judge sides with gold on the separating world) | NLI-DeBERTa pairwise | TVJT standalone (score<τ*) | B1 standalone (score<τ*) |\n|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| NEG | 300 | 0.838 | 0.788 (53 undistinguishable) | 0.957 | 0.990 |\n| QUANT | 141 | 0.883 | 0.709 (12 undistinguishable) | 0.950 | 0.957 |\n| IMPL_REV | 121 | 0.864 | 0.703 (30 undistinguishable) | 0.984 | 0.992 |\n| DROP_CONJ | 82 | 0.860 | 0.677 (35 undistinguishable) | 0.744 | 0.671 |\n| ADD_CONJ | 300 | 0.863 | 0.582 (213 undistinguishable) | 0.890 | 0.803 |\n| ARG_SWAP | 81 | 0.883 | 0.735 (27 undistinguishable) | 0.901 | 0.407 |\n| AND_OR | 94 | 0.830 | 0.702 (22 undistinguishable) | 0.957 | 0.947 |\n| SCOPE_SWAP | 0 | — | — (— undistinguishable) | — | — |\n| MERGE | 40 | 0.825 | 0.713 (13 undistinguishable) | 0.325 | 0.575 |\n| CARD | 28 | 0.762 | 0.679 (14 undistinguishable) | 0.929 | 0.893 |\n\n- Judge oracle accuracy on gold worlds: 0.8555. Mean score on gold:\n  NLI-DeBERTa 0.8195, NLI-gemini 0.9385.\n- Error-type identification (argmax operator, 10 classes), macro-F1: TVJT 0.2525\n  (top-1 0.3564); NLI 0.1814 (top-1 0.2115).\n- ADD_CONJ with a fresh predicate on ground formulas is invisible to NLI, because the hypotheses use only F's own\n  vocabulary.\n- **FOLIO-dev gold has zero mixed ∀/∃ formulas**, so SCOPE_SWAP never applies on the screen.\n\n**Blind-spot head-to-head** (`results/blindspot_headtohead.json`; Arm A's signature predicted ≈ chance):\n\n| metric / op / split | detection | n | Wilson 95% |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| TVJT / SCOPE_SWAP / screen | — | 0 | [None, None] |\n| TVJT / SCOPE_SWAP / screen+supplement | 0.649 | 37 | [0.4876, 0.7818] |\n| TVJT / CARD / screen | 0.762 | 84 | [0.6606, 0.8403] |\n| TVJT / CARD / screen+supplement | 0.662 | 468 | [0.6184, 0.7038] |\n| NLI_deberta / SCOPE_SWAP / screen | — | 0 | [None, None] |\n| NLI_deberta / SCOPE_SWAP / screen+supplement | 0.473 | 37 | [0.3224, 0.6287] |\n| NLI_deberta / CARD / screen | 0.643 | 56 | [0.512, 0.7555] |\n| NLI_deberta / CARD / screen+supplement | 0.605 | 440 | [0.5582, 0.6492] |\n\n### Other reported quantities\n\n- **Coverage.** Uncovered items get 0.5 and stay in every AUROC: TVJT 0.9304,\n  NLI 0.9184, LC 0.9004, B3sc 0.5186.\n  World coverage is 1.0 of parseable targets, with 4.268 worlds\n  per target.\n- **Verbalizer failure.** 0.0698 of items and 0.0398\n  of predicates.\n- **Cost per item** (fresh-call $ | median s):\n  - TVJT $0.000191 | 0.687 s;\n  - B1 $0.000024 | 0.507 s;\n  - NLI-gemini $0.000482 | 0.96 s;\n  - NLI-DeBERTa $0 | 0.059 s;\n  - LC $0 (z3 pairwise equivalence done in the build stage).\n\n  **Total OpenRouter spend of this workspace: $1.5501**, covering dev checks, the mini\n  run and the full screen, out of a $10 budget with a $6.50 hard cap.\n- **Judge prompt checks.** Dev slice (T2, 20 non-screen FOLIO-dev sentences): judge oracle\n  accuracy 0.702 and 0% malformed JSON,\n  so the TVJT prompt v1 was frozen. Blind-spot probe pairs (T1): the judge sided with gold on\n  7/8.\n- **System level.** With 3 systems, τ is not reported. The ordering by metric mean is in\n  `analysis.json → system_level`.\n\n## Independent audit of headline numbers\n\n`audit/rederive.py` recomputes the headline numbers from the RAW files: `data/screen_set.json` labels,\n`results/screen_scores.jsonl` rows, `data/candidate_pairs.json` and the cost ledgers. It uses a different code path:\nsklearn `roc_auc_score`, a pandas cluster bootstrap, `cross_val_predict` OOF, brute-force within-sentence pairs, its\nown Youden loop and a direct MAJ recount. Output: `audit/rederive_out.json`.\n- **Matched exactly** (point estimates):\n  - AUROC: LC 0.853, TVJT 0.701, NLI 0.603, B1 0.665, and all terciles;\n  - G3 deltas: LC +0.111, TVJT +0.039, NLI +0.001;\n  - within-sentence accuracy: LC 0.765, TVJT 0.729, B1 0.593;\n  - TVJT − B1 on the top tercile: +0.199;\n  - TVJT G2 false alarms: 0.479 at τ*=1.0 and 0.184 at 0.5;\n  - labels 463/370, vocab-mismatch share 0.676, LC_maj (0 mismatches), spend $1.55.\n- **Confidence intervals** differ only in the third decimal (different bootstrap RNG).\n- **Placebo:** with permuted labels, AUROC is 0.51–0.52 and LC's G3 CI is [−0.032, +0.043], so G3 correctly fails.\n- **Not independently re-derived:** the z3 labels themselves (the audit reuses them) and the LC_onecoin EM posteriors\n  (only their AUROC was re-derived).\n\n## Layout\n\n```\nmethod.py                 orchestrator: --stage build,b1,tvjt,nli,lc,tvjt_nv,analysis,export  [--limit N for mini runs]\nsrc/fol_core.py           tokenizer/parser (FOLIO unicode), finite grounding → z3, py_eval, bounded/unbounded equivalence,\n                          blind bijection labeller (fingerprint filter + z3), trigram-anchored labeller\nsrc/mutants.py            10 typed mutation operators + 5 meaning-preserving rewrites (z3-verified)\nsrc/build_screen.py       recipe: Logic-LM files × FOLIO v1 gold × curated gold → 300 sentences, labels, controls, supplement\nsrc/verbalize.py          deterministic fact verbalizer (WordNet/spaCy head category; gloss variant)\nsrc/tvjt.py               metric (ii): distinguishing-world search (z3 Optimize, minimal worlds), prompt, scoring\nsrc/instance_nli.py       metric (iii): consequence pairs, z3 E/C/N labels, DeBERTa agreement, error type\nsrc/latent_class.py       metric (iv): one-coin EM w/ all-wrong state, Hui–Walter, gold-as-rater, MAJ, Dawid–Skene, B3sc\nsrc/llm.py                OpenRouter client: sha1 disk cache, cost ledger, $6.50 breaker\nsrc/stages.py             stage implementations (B1/B2, TVJT, TVJT_nv, NLI, LC)\nsrc/analysis.py           AUROC + clustered CIs, gates G1–G4, detection, confusion, costs, robustness, selection rule, figures\nsrc/export.py             method_out.json (exp_gen_sol_out schema)\nsrc/dev_checks.py         T1 probe pairs + T2 dev-slice prompt check (then prompt frozen)\nsrc/integrity.py          T6 checks + cross-arm screen diff\nsrc/metrics_api.py        reusable (text, fol) functions: tvjt_score, instance_nli_score, latent_class_scores, parse_ok\naudit/rederive.py         independent re-derivation of headline numbers + placebo tests (audit/rederive_out.json)\nsrc/make_readme.py        regenerates README.md (Results section filled only from results/*.json; head/tail templates in src/readme_*.md)\ntests/test_core.py        T0 unit tests (parser, equivalence, reversed-implication flaw, py_eval≡z3, verbalizer, worlds, scope)\ndata/screen_set.json      sentences, real items + all labels, mutants, rewrites, counts, feature_spec, fingerprint\ndata/alt_candidates.jsonl duplicate per-example translations (self-consistency resource) with equivalence to primary\ndata/blindspot_supplement.jsonl  SCOPE_SWAP/CARD mutants (split = screen | supplement | supplement_folio_train | ...)\ndata/candidate_pairs.json pairwise equivalence of the 3 systems' candidates (blind + trigram maps)\ndata/worlds_cache.jsonl   TVJT worlds + prompts per target;  data/worlds_cache_nv.jsonl for TVJT_nv\ndata/consequences_cache.jsonl  NLI (premise, hypothesis) texts, z3 labels, mutant relabels per target\ndata/raw/                 downloaded public inputs (Logic-LM outputs, FOLIO v1 val/train, curated FOLIO/MALLS, MALLS-v0.1 test)\nresults/screen_scores.jsonl  one row per (item, metric): score, covered, error_type_pred, usd, seconds (+ per-world detail)\nresults/analysis.json     every reported number;  results/verdict.json gates + selection outcome\nresults/blindspot_headtohead.json, results/latent_class_info.json, results/integrity_checks.json, results/dev_checks.json\nresults/prompts_frozen.json, results/b1_request_example.json, results/cost_ledger*.jsonl\nfigures/                  auroc_by_tercile, per_operator_detection, confusion_TVJT / confusion_NLI_deberta (png/pdf)\nruns/mini_10/             T3 mini run (first 10 screen sentences), full pipeline outputs\nlogs/                     run logs; logs/repro/ = first-pass verdict + score hash for the cache-consistent re-run check\nmethod_out.json           exp_gen_sol_out export: every real/gold/mutant/rewrite item with labels + predict_<metric>\ncache/llm/                sha1-keyed They are derived from the 10 kept source datasets. `heldout_confirm` is the primary confirmation set; `heldout_samples` supports self-consistency baselines; `screen_gold_audit` gives audited labels joinable to the iter-1 screen; `contamination` supports the gold-recall check; `transfer_unlabeled` holds the user's domain.\n\n| Group | Rows | Content |\n|---|---|---|\n| `heldout_confirm` | 6,300 | 700 sentences × 9 systems, greedy (T=0). Unparseable outputs are kept (301, `parse_ok=false`) |\n| `heldout_samples` | 7,000 | 700 × 5 samples (T=0.8, seed=idx) × {gpt-4.1-mini, llama-3.1-8b}. L1 labels only |\n| `screen_gold_audit` | 1,003 | Screen reproduction: sha1-first-360 FOLIO-v1-validation sentences (300 + 60 buffer, `metadata_in_screen_first300`) × Logic-LM {gpt-3.5-turbo, gpt-4, text-davinci-003} candidates, labelled against panel-audited gold. Join key: `metadata_sentence_id` = sha1(norm)[:10]; `metadata_item_id` = sha1[:10]:system |\n| `contamination` | 936 | E1: 104 kept entity-renamed paraphrases × 9 renamed greedy candidates. Label transferred from the original candidate; the original sentence is kept for gold-recall probes |\n| `transfer_unlabeled` | 367 | E2: EU-AI-Act Art. 3 definitions × {grounded, ungrounded} × runs from the user pilot. `output='unlabeled'`. 289 parse; the 66 `⊆` formulas stay `parse_ok=false` |\n\nSample composition:\n- **MALLS 250**, including all 99 eligible paper-corrected items;\n- **FOLIO-train 250**, from stories disjoint from both FOLIO validation versions (5 stories excluded, overlap asserted 0);\n- **ProverQA hard 120 / medium 80**, de-templated, ≤2 per story, non-trivial.\n\nPer-corpus terciles are 45/30/25% (top/middle/bottom). Complexity features follow the screen recipe:\n- n_tokens;\n- n_quantifiers;\n- nesting depth;\n- n_conditions = if/unless/except/only/either/neither/not (with n't counted as not) + gold → ↔ ⊕ ∨ ¬.\n\nThe composite is the mean of the 4 z-scores over the 4,527-sentence eligible pool. The z-parameters and cut-points are in the dataset metadata and `label_report.json`.\n\n## 4. Row schema (`heldout_confirm` / `heldout_samples`)\n\n- **`input`**: a JSON string `{\"sentence\", \"candidate_fol\"}`, where `candidate_fol` is the extracted formula line.\n- **`output`**: `faithful` | `unfaithful` | `unknown`.\n\nFinal-label precedence:\n1. **L3 panel majority** (`label_source=panel3`, 609 greedy rows).\n2. For ProverQA with gold the panel judged faithful: **L1 vs the original gold** (`equiv_synthetic_gold`).\n3. Otherwise: **L1 vs audited gold** (`equiv_audited_gold`). `equiv_proved`/`equiv_bounded` map to faithful; `non_equiv`/`non_equiv_no_bijection` map to unfaithful.\n4. Otherwise `unknown` (453 greedy rows): unparseable candidates, `no_audited_gold` (the panel's correction failed to parse or verify) and `unknown_capped`. Unknown rows are kept and counted.\n\nMain metadata fields:\n- **Provenance**: `corpus`, `corpus_subset`, `source_id`, `story_id`, `licence`, `system`, `model`, `system_tier` (required/extra), `provider`, `sample_idx`, `temperature`, `raw_output`, `parse_ok`, `parse_error`, `parse_notes`, `finish_reason`, `prompt_sha1`, `extractor_version`, `gen_usd`, `gen_seconds`.\n- **Gold**:\n  - `gold_fol_original`, `gold_fol_audited`;\n  - `gold_source` ∈ {original, panel_corrected, panel_flagged_uncorrected (the panel flagged the gold but no correction parsed/verified, so `gold_fol_audited`=null and the label is unknown unless L3-adjudicated), paper_corrected}. In the greedy rows: original 3,087, panel_corrected 2,160, paper_corrected 891, panel_flagged_uncorrected 162;\n  - `gold_fol_paper` + `paper_corrected_flag` (the 2606.02837 human correction);\n  - `gold_fol_refined` (yfxiao/folio-refined, metadata only).\n- **L0**: `gold_audit_votes` (per member: faithful, ambiguous, error types, explanation, model), `gold_faithful_final`, `sentence_ambiguous`, `gold_audit_primary_error`, `gold_audit_cascade_rule`, `gold_audit_full_panel_subset`, `gold_correction_status`.\n- **L1**, for both `L1_orig_*` and `L1_audited_*`:\n  - `status` ∈ {equiv_proved, equiv_bounded, non_equiv, non_equiv_no_bijection, unknown_capped, unknown_timeout, unparseable, no_audited_gold};\n  - `mapping` (gold→candidate bijection);\n  - `entail_cand_to_gold` / `entail_gold_to_cand` (bounded one-directional hints under the best mapping);\n  - `method` (bounded/unbounded), `max_domain`, `seconds`.\n- **L2**: `L2_status` ∈ {equiv_granular, non_equiv, not_applicable, not_run_equivalent}, `L2_definitions`, `L2_lexical=true`, `L2_orig_status`.\n- **L3**:\n  - `L3_selected`;\n  - `L3_votes` (per member: cand_faithful, gold_faithful, error_types, primary_error, ambiguous, same_meaning, model);\n  - `L3_majority`, `L3_primary_error`, `L3_dissent`, `L3_gold_shown_as`;\n  - `L3_sampling_weight` (post-stratified N_h/n_h), `L3_poststratum`, `L3_design_weight`, `L3_design_stratum`;\n  - `L3_stratum_p_faithful` (estimated panel-faithful share of this row's post-stratum; a label-noise prior for non-adjudicated rows).\n- **Complexity**: `n_tokens`, `n_quantifiers`, `nesting_depth`, `n_conditions`, `complexity_composite`, `complexity_tercile`, `complexity_gold_used`.\n- **`label_source`**; **`source_verified`** / **`source_file`** (set by `data.py`).\n\n## 5. How labels were built\n\n**Panel.** Every member is family-disjoint from all 9 generators (Meta, Qwen, Mistral, OpenAI, Google, DeepSeek, Microsoft) and from the gemini judge baseline.\n\n| Member | Model | Calibration gate (40 known-label items, ≥80% required) | Note |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| M1 | anthropic/claude-haiku-4.5 | 0.90 | The planned claude-sonnet-4.5 **failed** at 0.775: it rejected valid contrapositive and material-implication rewrites. The plan's fallback was used |\n| M2 | x-ai/grok-4.3 (reasoning low) | 0.90 | grok-4-fast is no longer in the OpenRouter catalog |\n| M3 | z-ai/glm-4.6, **non-thinking** | 0.875 | Chosen for cost; the low-reasoning config scored 0.925 |\n\nThe calibration set:\n- 15 ProverQA-easy golds;\n- 15 solver-verified non-equivalent mutants: negation flip, ∀↔∃, implication reversal, dropped conjunct, and ∧↔∨ (used because ProverQA-easy has no binary predicates for argument swaps);\n- 10 solver-verified equivalent rewrites: contrapositive, material implication, predicate renaming.\n\nThe gate uses the original, pre-registered labels, even though 3 of the \"faithful\" golds are defensibly wrong.\n\n**L0 / L4 gold audit.** A cascade: M2 and M3 vote on every gold; M1 votes on disagreements plus a seeded 20% subset. There were 264 M1 tie-breaks among 1,060 golds.\n\nA correction is accepted only if it:\n- parses;\n- is satisfiable (bounded);\n- has a bounded countermodel against the original.\n\nOtherwise it is rejected: 33 corrections were equivalent to the original, and some were unparseable because they used function terms. For the 99 MALLS items with a human correction, `gold_fol_audited` = the paper's gold.\n\n**L1** is lexical-free.\n- It checks the arity profile first. A mismatch gives `non_equiv_no_bijection`.\n- It then tries all bijections, capped at 5,040 (only 2 greedy items hit the cap).\n- Only countermodels count as proofs of non-equivalence.\n- `equiv_proved` needs an unbounded z3 `unsat`. All equivalences in this run were proved, so no `equiv_bounded` items occur.\n\nValidation on the 59 parseable pairs among the paper's first 60 MALLS items: every uncorrected pair was equivalent (37/37) and every corrected pair was non-equivalent (22/22). Mean cost is about 6 ms per pair.\n\n**L3** is blinded and symmetric: gold and candidate appear as Formula A / Formula B in seeded random order, with no source or system named.\n- The design sample was 460: 400 non-equivalent, with the top tercile oversampled 1.5×, including 40 of the 77 L2-granular items; plus 60 equivalent.\n- 150 extra items were drawn uniformly at random within the top-tercile non-equivalent strata.\n- Estimates use post-stratified weights on the final frame. The frame has 5,848 greedy parseable candidates with audited gold; every stratum has at least 3 adjudicated items.\n\n**E1.**\n- 203 sentences carried a named entity or constant: FOLIO 187, MALLS only 16, because MALLS gold is almost constant-free. So the planned 75/75 split became 187/16.\n- gpt-4.1-mini paraphrased each sentence and renamed its entities. The renaming was applied to the gold constants and predicate substrings.\n- M2 verified both the meaning and the renamed gold. 104 of 203 passed (target was ≥120). Most drops are \"the renamed gold is not faithful to the paraphrase\", which reflects residual gold problems.\n- 124 of 936 renamed candidates are flagged `contamination_rename_incomplete`.\n\n## 6. Deviations from the plan (honest list)\n\n1. **Corrected gold WAS found.** arXiv:2606.02837's release is on HF as `DSAVlab-UNIUD/MALLS_test_subset-CURATED` (100 items = MALLS-test indices 0–99, verified by NL match) and `DSAVlab-UNIUD/FOLIO_validation-curated` (275 FOLIO-v2-validation instances: 73 whole-story premise blocks plus 202 conclusions). The paper's GitHub repo is still 404.\n   - The FOLIO file corrects stories and v2 conclusions, not v1 premises. So the L4 panel-vs-human check covers only the 22 screen conclusions whose v1 gold equals the v2 gold the paper judged (κ = 0.18, n = 22: too small to conclude anything).\n2. **Panel substitutions**: haiku-4.5 for sonnet-4.5 (failed the gate), grok-4.3 for the unavailable grok-4-fast, glm-4.6 non-thinking. See §5.\n3. **ProverQA was audited in full** (the 10% escalation rule fired). Its flagged golds use panel-corrected gold (`gold_source=panel_corrected`).\n4. **L3 was enlarged** from 300 to 610 adjudicated items (460 design + 150 extra top-tercile), funded from the reserve. Weights are post-stratified.\n5. **A parser bug was found and fixed after L0.** v1 read constants such as `yr2028`/`p1080` as free variables and universally closed them. It affected 8 golds (4 held-out) and 9 candidates.\n   - The drawn sample was kept frozen.\n   - The 4 affected sentences' complexity features were recomputed with the stored z-parameters (the old values are in `complexity_v1_buggy`).\n   - All L1/L2 labels were recomputed from scratch; the old cache is kept as `work/l1_cache_v1_buggy.jsonl`.\n   - The panel corrections were re-derived (0 changed).\n   - E1 verification was re-keyed on content. This re-called all 203 verifications, costing $0.49 of waste.\n   - 3 L3 items changed stratum; post-stratification absorbs this.\n6. **Extractor v2**: phi-4 answered in LaTeX (`\\(\\forall x \\, \\text{P}(x)\\)`). A formatting fix, not a prompt change, raised its parse rate from 68% to 88%. All rows were re-extracted from the stored `raw_output`.\n7. **The ASCII `^` is read as ∧, not ⊕**, because LLM outputs use it as a look-alike of ∧.\n8. The **folio-refined** signal barely agrees with the panel: κ = 0.004 on 416 FOLIO items; refined differs from the original on 34%, the panel flags 51%. Its (undocumented) refinement targets something other than faithfulness, e.g. cross-story vocabulary alignment. It is kept as metadata only.\n9. **Class balance**: 62% of labelled greedy rows are unfaithful, above the 25–60% target. This is inflated by the L1 label noise described in §1. Per corpus: ProverQA 42%, MALLS 66%, FOLIO 75%.\n10. **\"Heavily conditioned\" means connective-heavy, not exception-heavy.** The public sentence-level gold corpora contain almost no `unless`/`except` sentences, so the top tercile is driven by multiple connectives, ⊕, ↔ and quantifier nesting. E2 (EU AI Act) is the only exception-rich material, and it is unlabeled.\n11. Temperature-0 output is not bit-deterministic across OpenRouter providers. The provider is recorded per row, and `raw_output` is the ground truth of what was scored.\n12. `kenken6696/folio_by_ccg2lambda` and `kenken6696/MALLS_by_ccg2lambda` were downloaded (to `temp/datasets/`) as possible iter-2 non-LLM systems but **not used**. Their neo-Davidsonian event FOL has no predicate bijection to gold, so they could only be adjudicated by the panel.\n\n## 7. Dataset search and selection (TODOs 2–5)\n\n**Search.** 40 broad HF queries (`temp/hf_search.txt`), including: folio, MALLS, ProverQA, first-order logic, FOL, autoformalization, logical reasoning, semantic parsing, ProofWriter, LogicNLI, theorem proving, lambda calculus, monotonicity, logic-lm, …\n\n**Previews.** 20 candidates were previewed through the HF datasets-server API (`temp/previews/candidate_previews.json`; the skill's own preview script lacked the `datasets` module). 14 provenance web searches are in `temp/websearch/`.\n\n**KEPT (10):**\n\n| Dataset | Downloads | Licence | Role |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| tasksource/folio | 6.2k | FOLIO MIT | FOLIO-v2 train/validation (yale-nlp/FOLIO is gated for this token) |\n| yuan-yang/MALLS-v0 | 158 | CC BY-NC 4.0 | MALLS-v0.1-test (Yang et al. 2023) |\n| DSAVlab-UNIUD/MALLS_test_subset-CURATED | 26 | — | Human corrections from arXiv:2606.02837. Low downloads, but provenance is verified: card, paper and authors |\n| DSAVlab-UNIUD/FOLIO_validation-curated | 61 | — | Same paper; FOLIO side |\n| opendatalab/ProverQA | 380 | — | ICLR 2025, dev easy/medium/hard via `hf_hub_download`; `load_dataset` breaks |\n| yfxiao/folio-refined | 20 | MIT | Independent wrong-gold signal (method undocumented; metadata only) |\n| Yale-LILY/FOLIO GitHub v0.0 | — | — | v1 validation/train (screen gold) |\n| teacherpeterpan/Logic-LLM `outputs/logic_programs/FOLIO_dev_*.json` | — | — | Screen candidates |\n| User `dpv_pilot_study.zip` | — | — | E2 |\n| kenken6696/{folio,MALLS}_by_ccg2lambda | — | CC BY 4.0 | Downloaded for iter 2, unused |\n\n**DISCARDED**, each with its reason:\n\n| Dataset | Reason |\n|---|---|\n| benlipkin/folio, minimario/FOLIO, jhkim64/NL2FOL_sentence | Duplicate FOLIO mirrors, or <10 downloads with no card |\n| tasksource/FOL-nli (182 MB), tasksource/LogicNLI, tasksource/proofwriter, logicreasoning/logi_glue (963 MB), kkkarry/LogicGraph (698 MB) | NLI/QA with TPTP or proof annotations; no sentence-level NL→FOL gold, and some are too large |\n| Isotonic/symbolic_data_first_order_logic | Arithmetic \"FOL\"; no card |\n| verify-ppt/ppt-logic_first_order | No loadable data |\n| tasksource/monotonicity-entailment (MED) | Used by screen Arm A, not a FOL corpus |\n\n## 8. How to run (in order)\n\n```bash\nuv venv .venv --python=3.12 && uv pip install --python=.venv/bin/python z3-solver nltk aiohttp loguru huggingface_hub numpy scipy tenacity requests pyyaml pytest\nbash restore.sh                                    # sources + nltk data + pilot extraction\n.venv/bin/python -m pytest -q -c pytest.ini tests/test_fol.py\n.venv/bin/python src/prep_sources.py               # steps 1-3 (no API)\n.venv/bin/python src/generate.py --concurrency 24  # step 4 (~$0.72; resumable)\n.venv/bin/python src/reextract.py                  # extractor v2\n.venv/bin/python src/label_l1.py --stage orig\n.venv/bin/python src/calibrate.py && .venv/bin/python src/calibrate_variants.py   # (~$0.88)\n.venv/bin/python src/audit.py --cap 4.0            # L0 + L4 (~$3.43)\n.venv/bin/python src/fix_varlike_patch.py          # only needed for artefacts produced by parser v1\n.venv/bin/python src/label_l1.py --stage audited\n.venv/bin/python src/l3_adjudicate.py --n-non 400 --n-eq 60 --cap 2.8   # (~$2.46)\n.venv/bin/python src/l3_extra.py --n 150 --cap 0.85                      # (~$0.88)\n.venv/bin/python src/e1_contamination.py --n 203 --cap 0.75              # (~$0.86)\n.venv/bin/python src/e2_transfer.py\n.venv/bin/python src/assemble.py                  # -> work/assembled_data_out.json.gz + label_report.json\nuv run data.py                                     # source verification -> full_data_out.json (validated: exp_sel_data_out)\n.venv/bin/python src/split_outputs.py             # -> full_data_out/full_data_out_{1,2,3}.json + mini/preview\n```\n\nEvery panel and generation response is cached (`work/panel_cache.jsonl`, `raw/generations/`), so a re-run of the labelling steps costs $0.\n\nTotal spend: **$9.22**:\n\n| Phase | Cost |\n|---|---|\n| Generation | $0.72 |\n| Calibration | $0.88 |\n| Gold audit | $3.43 |\n| L3 | $2.46 + $0.88 |\n| E1 | $0.86 |\n\nIt stayed under the $9.30 hard cap, with the ledger updated after every call.\n\n## 9. Restoring removed files\n\nThese paths are deleted after the round (see `.aii/manifest.yaml`). `bash restore.sh` recreates all of them. It also re-downloads `raw/hf_downloads/` and the ccg2lambda files in `temp/datasets/` if they are missing; those are small and are kept.\n\n| Path | Restore with |\n|---|---|\n| `raw/pilot/` | `python -c \"import zipfile; zipfile.ZipFile('/ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_qY2a2IS-WLIs/user_uploads/dpv_pilot_study.zip').extractall('raw/pilot')\"` |\n| `nltk_data/` | `python -c \"import nltk; [nltk.download(p, download_dir='nltk_data') for p in ('wordnet','omw-1.4')]\"` |\n| `.venv/` | The `uv venv …` line in §8 |\n| `__pycache__/`, `.pytest_cache/` | Regenerated automatically |\n29017 label_report.json", "stderr": "\nShell cwd was reset to /ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_qY2a2IS-WLIs/3_invention_loop/iter_1/gen_report_text/gen_report_text", "interrupted": false, "isImage": false, "noOutputExpected": false}

### [48] TOOL CALL — Bash · 2026-09-23 13:31:02 UTC

```
cd /ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_qY2a2IS-WLIs/3_invention_loop/iter_1/gen_art; mkdir -p /ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_qY2a2IS-WLIs/3_invention_loop/iter_1/gen_report_text/gen_report_text/checks; cat > /ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_qY2a2IS-WLIs/3_invention_loop/iter_1/gen_report_text/gen_report_text/checks/recompute_auroc.py <<'EOF'
"""Recompute headline real-candidate AUROCs of both screen arms directly from per-item score rows.
Uncovered items keep the score the arm wrote (0.5 by the arms' rule). Mann-Whitney AUROC, ties = 1/2."""
import json, collections
BASE='/ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_qY2a2IS-WLIs/3_invention_loop/iter_1/gen_art/'
def auroc(pairs):
    pos=[s for s,y in pairs if y==1]; neg=[s for s,y in pairs if y==0]
    c=0.0
    for p in pos:
        for n in neg:
            c+= 1 if p>n else (0.5 if p==n else 0)
    return c/(len(pos)*len(neg)), len(pos), len(neg)
out={}
# Arm A
sA=json.load(open(BASE+'gen_art_experiment_1/data/screen_set.json'))
lab={r['item_id']:r['correct'] for r in sA['real']}
terc={s['sid']:s.get('tercile') for s in sA['sentences']}
sc=collections.defaultdict(dict)
for line in open(BASE+'gen_art_experiment_1/results/screen_scores.jsonl'):
    r=json.loads(line)
    if r['item_id'] in lab: sc[r['metric']][r['item_id']]=r['score']
for m in ['A1','A2','A3','A0','Ccov','B1','B3nli','B3cos','B4','B5','B7','B8','B2']:
    if m not in sc: continue
    pr=[(sc[m].get(i,0.5) if sc[m].get(i) is not None else 0.5,y) for i,y in lab.items()]
    a,p,n=auroc(pr)
    top=[(sc[m].get(i,0.5) if sc[m].get(i) is not None else 0.5,y) for i,y in lab.items() if terc.get(i.split(':')[0])=='top']
    out['A:'+m]=dict(auroc=round(a,4),n_pos=p,n_neg=n,top=round(auroc(top)[0],4) if top else None, n_scored=len(sc[m]))
# Arm B
sB=json.load(open(BASE+'gen_art_experiment_2/data/screen_set.json'))
labB={r['item_id']:1 if r['L_bij']=='correct' else 0 for r in sB['real_items']}
scB=collections.defaultdict(dict)
for line in open(BASE+'gen_art_experiment_2/results/screen_scores.jsonl'):
    r=json.loads(line)
    if r['item_id'] in labB: scB[r['metric']][r['item_id']]=r['score']
for m in ['LC_onecoin','TVJT','NLI_deberta','B1','B2','B3sc','NLI_gemini','TVJT_nv','LC_maj']:
    if m not in scB: continue
    pr=[(scB[m].get(i,0.5) if scB[m].get(i) is not None else 0.5,y) for i,y in labB.items()]
    a,p,n=auroc(pr)
    out['B:'+m]=dict(auroc=round(a,4),n_pos=p,n_neg=n,n_scored=len(scB[m]))
# shared items
shared=set(lab)&set(labB)
out['shared_items']=len(shared)
out['label_agreement_on_shared']=round(sum(lab[i]==labB[i] for i in shared)/len(shared),4)
# B1 on shared under each arm's labels
for nm,L,S in [('A',lab,sc['B1']),('B',labB,scB['B1'])]:
    out['B1_shared_'+nm+'labels']=round(auroc([(S.get(i,0.5),L[i]) for i in shared])[0],4)
out['B1_armA_scores_vs_armB_labels_shared']=round(auroc([(sc['B1'].get(i,0.5),labB[i]) for i in shared])[0],4)
json.dump(out,open('/ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_qY2a2IS-WLIs/3_invention_loop/iter_1/gen_report_text/gen_report_text/checks/recompute_auroc_out.json','w'),indent=1)
print(json.dumps(out,indent=1))
EOF
python3 /ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_qY2a2IS-WLIs/3_invention_loop/iter_1/gen_report_text/gen_report_text/checks/recompute_auroc.py
```

cd /ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_qY2a2IS-WLIs/3_invention_loop/iter_1/gen_report_text/gen_report_text/checks; cat > cross_arm.py <<'EOF'
"""Cross-arm check on the 803 real items both screen arms share: every metric scored under BOTH arms' labels."""
import json, collections
BASE='/ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_qY2a2IS-WLIs/3_invention_loop/iter_1/gen_art/'
def auroc(pairs):
    pos=[s for s,y in pairs if y==1]; neg=[s for s,y in pairs if y==0]
    return sum((1 if p>n else 0.5 if p==n else 0) for p in pos for n in neg)/(len(pos)*len(neg))
sA=json.load(open(BASE+'gen_art_experiment_1/data/screen_set.json'))
labA={r['item_id']:r['correct'] for r in sA['real']}
sB=json.load(open(BASE+'gen_art_experiment_2/data/screen_set.json'))
labB={r['item_id']:1 if r['L_bij']=='correct' else 0 for r in sB['real_items']}
sc=collections.defaultdict(dict)
for f,pre in [('gen_art_experiment_1','A:'),('gen_art_experiment_2','B:')]:
    for line in open(BASE+f+'/results/screen_scores.jsonl'):
        r=json.loads(line); sc[pre+r['metric']][r['item_id']]=r['score']
shared=sorted(set(labA)&set(labB))
out={'n_shared':len(shared),'pos_A':sum(labA[i] for i in shared),'pos_B':sum(labB[i] for i in shared)}
for m in ['A:A1','A:A3','A:A0','A:B4','A:B5','A:B1','A:B3nli','A:B8','B:LC_onecoin','B:TVJT','B:NLI_deberta','B:B1','B:B3sc']:
    g=lambda i:(sc[m].get(i) if sc[m].get(i) is not None else 0.5)
    out[m]={'armA_labels':round(auroc([(g(i),labA[i]) for i in shared]),4),'armB_labels':round(auroc([(g(i),labB[i]) for i in shared]),4)}
json.dump(out,open('cross_arm_out.json','w'),indent=1); print(json.dumps(out,indent=1))
EOF
python3 cross_arm.py

LLM-generated code via program selection using the LLM as a test oracle, based on the observation that LLMs serve more effectively as oracles when tasked with selecting the correct output from multiple choices.\n\n## Methodology\n\nThe method first generates distinguishing inputs that capture semantic discrepancies of programs sampled from an LLM and records outputs produced by the programs on these inputs. In this paper, we conduct\na comprehensive study on the sensitivity of ex-\nisting NL-, FOL-, and graph-based metrics to\ncapture differences between a sampled FOL\nand its corresponding ground-truth. We then\nmeasure the alignment between a metric-based\nranking of FOL outputs and a strong LLM as-\na-judge. To do this, we first apply operator\nand text-based perturbations to ground-truth\nFOL statements to assess metric sensitivity. We\nthen evaluate metric robustness by comparing\nthe metrics against LLMs judgment. Our em-\npirical findings highlight a clear oversensitiv-\nity in the n-gram metric BLEU for text per-\nturbations. The operator perturbation affects\nthe semantic graph metric Smatch++ for struc-\ntural changes, and the FOL metric for specific\noperator changes. We observe a closer align-\nment between BertScore and LLM judgement,\nproving the importance of semantic evaluation.\nAdditionally, we show that combining metrics\nenhances both robustness and sensitivity com-\npared to using individual metrics. 1\n1\nIntroduction\nLarge language models (LLMs) have advanced nat-\nural language reasoning, but logical and mathemati-\ncal reasoning have long relied on formal, structured\nlanguages for proving deductions and theorems, a\nprocess that predates deep neural networks (Can-\ndela et al., 2006). This approach remains relevant\ntoday, especially for reasoning tasks that can be\n1Our\ncode\nis\navailable\nat\nhttps://github.com/\nRamyaKeerthy/AlignmentFOL\nsolved using formal statements. In case of first-\norder logic (FOL), LLM generations are used as\nintermediate steps and subsequently passed to theo-\nrem provers to solve the problem (Pan et al., 2023;\nYe et al., 2023; Olausson et al., 2023). Compared to\nthe Chain-of-Thought (CoT) approach (Wei et al.,\n2022), where the model first reasons and then\nsolves, FOL generation demonstrated superior re-\nliability by offloading the reasoning task to an ex-\nternal tool. Translating natural language (NL) into\nFOL enhanced the overall rigor of the process.\nGenerating FOL from NL is a challenging task\nthat tests the ability of LLMs to accurately inter-\npret and convert informal language into a formal,\nstructured token sequence (Yang et al., 2024a; Wu\net al., 2022). The lack of ground truth for FOL gen-\nerations complicates direct verification. Yang et al.\n(2024b) addressed this challenge by developing a\nsystem specifically for FOL generation, incorporat-\ning an operator-based evaluator to rate the outputs.\nThis evaluation is combined with BLEU score, us-\ning a threshold as a metric in a reward model. How-\never, the reliance on thresholds complicates the\ninterpretation of translation quality. Manually as-\nsessing formal logic generations is labor-intensive\nand has received relatively less attention compared\nto traditional text translation metrics.\nIn this work, we analyze existing natural lan-\nguage translation, tree, and graph evaluation met-\nrics, focusing on those that offer strong sentence-\nlevel comparisons. We establish a framework to\nsystematically introduce perturbations and analyze\nthe existing metrics in the presence of these anoma-\nlies in formal language, specifically first-order\nlogic. Metric sensitivity helps reveal the degree\nto which each metric responds to specific types of\nperturbations. To further assess these metrics on\nreal-world examples, we generate sample FOLs\nfor NL statements in FOLIO (Han et al., 2024)\nand MALLS (Yang et al., 2024b) datasets using\nan LLM and rank them against ground truth val-\n1\narXiv:2501.08613v3  [cs.CL]  5 Sep 2025\n\nues. The ranking is conducted using established\nmetrics, and LLM-based evaluators. After a small-\nscale experiment to select the LLM most aligned\nwith human judgment for this task, we measure\nhow well each metric-based ranking aligns with\nthe selected LLM’s judgments 2. Our experiments\nreveal that BertScore has a better alignment as an\nindividual metric, and its combination with other\nmetrics could further boost its alignment. Our find-\nings provide insights into the sensitivity of current\nmetrics and their applicability to symbolic genera-\ntion tasks.\n2", "stderr": "", "interrupted": false, "isImage": false, "noOutputExpected": false}

{"stdout": "===== 2010.02840\nABS: Abstract We propose test suite accuracy to approximate semantic accuracy for Text-to-SQL models. Our method distills a small test suite of databases that achieves high code coverage for the gold query from a large number of randomly generated databases. At evaluation time, it computes the denotation accuracy of the predicted queries on the distilled test suite, hence calculating a tight upper-bound for semantic accuracy eﬃciently. We use our proposed method to evaluate 21 models submitted to the Spider leader board and manually verify that our method is always correct on 100 examples. In contrast, the current Spider metric leads to a 2.5% false negative rate on average and 8.1% in the worst case, indicating that test suite accuracy is needed. Our implementation, along with distilled test suites for eleven Textto-SQL datasets, is publicly available.1 1 \nINTRO: Introduction A Text-to-SQL model translates natural language instructions to SQL queries that can be executed on databases and bridges the gap between expert programmers and non-experts. Accordingly, researchers have built a diversity of datasets (Dahl, 1989; Iyer et al., 2017; Zhong et al., 2017; Yu et al., 2018) and improved model performances (Xu et al., 2017; Suhr et al., 2018; Guo et al., 2019; Bogin et al., 2019a; Wang et al., 2020). However, evaluating the semantic accuracy of a Text-to-SQL model is a long-standing problem: we want to know whether the predicted SQL query has the same denotation as the gold for every possible database. “Single” denotation evaluation executes the predicted SQL query on one database and compares its denotation with that of the gold. It might create false positives, where a semantically diﬀerent 1Metric implementation and test suites available here, for datasets: Spider, CoSQL, SParC, Academic, Advising, ATIS, Geography, IMDB, Restaurants, Scholar and Yelp. Query: “Who is above 34 years old?” NAME AGE Alice 35 Bob 37 Test Suite “People” Databases database 1 Predicted 1 Gold SELECT NAME FROM People WHERE AGE > 34 SELECT NAME FROM People (missing WHERE) … Denotations: Gold: Alice, Bob Predicted 1: Alice, Bob Predicted 2: Alice, Bob NAME AGE Alic\n-- Results : Results We ﬁrst show that our test suite evaluation is reliable by verifying that the test suite distinguishes most neighbor queries, and always makes the correct judgement on 100 model-predicted queries we manually examined (Section 6.1). Then we use test suite accuracy as a proxy for semantic accuracy to calculate the error rate of the existing commonly used metrics (Section 6.2) and their correlations (Section 6.3). At last we discuss the computational eﬃciency of test suite evaluation (Section 6.4). 6.1 Reliability Distinguish Neighbor Queries. For each gold query in Spider, we generate on average 94 neighbor queries (Figure 2). We sample 1000 random databases for each database schema and run fuzzing (Section 4.2) to construct S g, which takes around a week on 16 CPUs. Figure 5 plots the fraction of neighbor queries that remain undistinguished after attempting t random databases. Checking single database denotation fails to distinguish 5% of the neighbor queries, and the curve stops decreasing after around 600 random databases; 1000 random databases can distinguish > 99% of the neighbor queries. A large number of random databases is necessary to achieve comprehensive code coverage. Figure 4 presents some typical neighbor queries that have the same denotations as the gold on a\n===== 2305.12295\nABS: Abstract Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown human-like reasoning abilities but still struggle with complex logical problems. This paper introduces a novel framework, LOGIC- LM, which integrates LLMs with symbolic solvers to improve logical problem-solving. Our method first utilizes LLMs to translate a natural language problem into a symbolic formulation. Afterward, a deterministic symbolic solver performs inference on the formulated problem. We also introduce a selfrefinement module, which utilizes the symbolic solver’s error messages to revise symbolic formalizations. We demonstrate LOGIC-LM’s effectiveness on five logical reasoning datasets: ProofWriter, PrOntoQA, FOLIO, LogicalDeduction, and AR-LSAT. On average, LOGIC- LM achieves a significant performance boost of 39.2% over using LLM alone with standard prompting and 18.4% over LLM with chain-ofthought prompting. Our findings suggest that LOGIC-LM, by combining LLMs with symbolic logic, offers a promising avenue for faithful logical reasoning. 1 1 \nINTRO: Introduction Logical reasoning is a cognitive process that involves using evidence, arguments, and logic to arrive at conclusions or make judgments (Huang and Chang, 2023). It plays a central role in intelligent systems for problem-solving, decision-making, and critical thinking. Recently, large language models (LLMs) (Brown et al., 2020; Ouyang et al., 2022a; OpenAI, 2023) have exhibited emergent ability to “reason” like human (Wei et al., 2022a). When prompted with step-wise explanations of reasoning (“chain of thoughts”), or a simple prompt “Let’s think step by step.”, these models are able to answer questions with explicit reasoning steps (Wei et al., 2022b; Kojima et al., 2022). 1Code and data are publicly available at https://github. com/teacherpeterpan/Logic-LLM. Problem Formulator Symbolic Reasoner Result Interpreter Symbolic Formulation Symbolic Result Problem Goal Answer Self- Refine Figure 1: Overview of our LOGIC-LM framework. Despite the advances of LLMs, they still struggle with complex logical reasoning problems (Liu et al., 2023b). Recent studies (Golovneva et al., 2023; Ribeiro et al., 2023b; Lyu et al., 2023) found that LLMs occasionally make unfaithful reasoning, i.e., the derived conclusion does not follow the previously generated reasoning chain. While chain-\n-- Limitations : Limitations We identify two main limitations of LOGIC-LM. First, LOGIC-LM relies on translating reasoning problems into logical formats that can be tackled by symbolic solvers. As a consequence, the model’s applicability is inherently bounded by the expressiveness of the symbolic solver, for example, not all problems can be easily encoded in first-order logic. Nevertheless, this limitation can be mitigated by integrating a more diverse set of symbolic solvers. The flexible design of LOGIC-LM facilitates this integration. The wide range of reasoning tasks that we can instantiate our LOGIC-LM framework on shows its general applicability. Second, LOGIC-LM depends on in-context learning coupled with self-refinement to convert a natural language (NL) problem into the symbolic representation. While this method has proven to be effective, it may face difficulties when dealing with logical representations with intricate grammar structures, such as probabilistic soft logic. This arises from the difficulty in conveying complex grammatical rules to the language model through a limited number of demonstrations within a constrained context size. As a potential solution, future works could explore the development of specialized modules to enhance the mapping between NL and symbolic language, e\n-- Main Results : Main Results We report the results of LOGIC-LM (without selfrefinement) and baselines in Table 2. For LOGIC- LM, a symbolic solver does not return an answer when there are grammar errors in the symbolic formulation. For these un-executable cases, we fall back on using chain-of-thought to predict the answer. We have three major observations. 1. Logic-LM significantly outperforms standard LLMs and CoT across all datasets. With GPT- 3.5, our method outperforms standard LLM on all datasets, with an average improvement of 39.2%. This highlights the benefit of combining LLMs with external symbolic solvers for logical reasoning. LOGIC-LM also improves CoT by a large margin of 18.4% on average, showing that offloading the reasoning to symbolic solvers greatly improves faithfulness compared with pure language-based reasoning with CoT. 2. GPT-4 outperforms GPT-3.5 by a large margin of 48.46% on average for the standard prompting. Although this may indicate that the logical reasoning capability can be boosted by scaling up the LLM, we observe that GPT-4 still makes numerous unfaithful reasoning errors. By delegating the reasoning to symbolic solvers, our method can\n===== 2310.15164\nABS: Abstract Logical reasoning, i.e., deductively inferring the truth value of a conclusion from a set of premises, is an important task for artificial intelligence with wide potential impacts on science, mathematics, and society. While many prompting-based strategies have been proposed to enable Large Language Models (LLMs) to do such reasoning more effectively, they still appear unsatisfactory, often failing in subtle and unpredictable ways. In this work, we investigate the validity of instead reformulating such tasks as modular neurosymbolic programming, which we call LINC: Logical Inference via Neurosymbolic Computation. In LINC, the LLM acts as a semantic parser, translating premises and conclusions from natural language to expressions in first-order logic. These expressions are then offloaded to an external theorem prover, which symbolically performs deductive inference. Leveraging this approach, we observe significant performance gains on FOLIO and a balanced subset of ProofWriter for three different models in nearly all experimental conditions we evaluate. On ProofWriter, augmenting the comparatively small open-source StarCoder+ (15.5B parameters) with LINC even outperforms GPT-3.5 and GPT-4 with Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting by an absolute 38% and 10%, respectively. When used with GPT-4, LINC scores 26% higher than CoT on ProofWriter while performing comparatively on FOLIO. Further analysis reveals that although both methods on average succeed roughly equally often on this dataset, they exhibit distinct and complementary failure modes. We thus provide promising evidence for how logical reasoning over natural language can be tackled through jointly leveraging LLMs alongside symbolic provers. All corresponding code is publicly available.1 ∗Author order randomized;\nINTRO: Introduction Widespread adoption of large language models (LLMs) such as GPT-3 (Brown et al., 2020), GPT- 4 (OpenAI, 2023), and PaLM (Chowdhery et al., 2022) have led to a series of remarkable successes in tasks ranging from text summarization to program synthesis. Some of these successes have encouraged the hypothesis that such models are able to flexibly and systematically reason (Huang and Chang, 2022), especially when using prompting strategies that explicitly encourage verbalizing intermediate reasoning steps before generating the final answer (Nye et al., 2021; Wei et al., 2022; Kojima et al., 2022; Wang et al., 2023b). However, this reasoning ability appears to be unreliable for tasks that require reasoning out of domain (Liang et al., 2022; Saparov et al., 2023), understanding negation (Anil et al., 2022), and following long reasoning chains (Dziri et al., 2023). Furthermore, while the standard approach of “scaling up” seems to improve performance across some reasoning domains, other domains, e.g., reasoning involving use of Modus Tollens, show no such improvements (McKenzie et al., 2022). These findings suggest that such models may be relying on approximate heuristics based on surface-level statistical patterns in reasoning tasks, rather than consistent, generalizable re\n-- Limitations : Limitations Narrow scope of logical reasoning task considered: In this work, we focus exclusively on one aspect of logical reasoning: predicting the truth value of a conclusion given a set of natural language premises. Here, we consider a setting where the premises and conclusion are expressed in relatively short statements, which makes the formalization task tractable. In particular, ProofWriter’s natural language statements are synthetically generated, so they can be easily and accurately parsed into FOL. FOLIO reflects a more naturalistic dataset, so we see a higher failure rate in LINC’s semantic parsing step. However, the formalization task becomes more difficult if the premises are in longer paragraph form, such as in question answering or contradiction detection from context passages. This is because the same piece of information can be formalized in a variety of ways, and there is a lot of information that must be pragmatically inferred to arrive at the proper conclusion. Generalizability of qualitative evaluation: While we find that LINC and CoT produce complementary mistakes for our natural language reasoning task, it is unclear if this result also holds true in similar scenarios, such as the ones considered in PAL (Gao et al., 2023), Logic-LM, and SATLM. To investigate this issue, we propose a Systematic Generalization testbed based on Natural language Semantics (SyGNS), whose challenge is to map natural language sentences to multiple forms of scoped meaning representations, designed to account for various semantic phenomena. Using SyGNS, we test whether neural networks can systematically parse sentences involving novel combinations of logical expressions such as quantiﬁers and negation. Experiments show that Transformer and GRU models can generalize to unseen combinations of quantiﬁers, negations, and modiﬁers that are similar to given training instances in form, but not to the others. We also ﬁnd that the generalization performance to unseen combinations is better when the form of meaning representations is simpler. These results indicate that models can easily generalize to problems involving quantiﬁers of the same type as the primitive quantiﬁer, while the models struggle with generalizing to the others. File: /ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_qY2a2IS-WLIs/3_invention_loop/iter_1/gen_report_text/gen_report_text/style_exemplars.md

How these papers write: sentences run from about 8 to 40 words, with long setup sentences broken by short verdicts ("We have three major observations."). "We" is used throughout for what the authors did. Numbers go into the sentence plainly with their baseline ("56.0% vs. 41.8% with CoT"). Hedging is rare and kept to interpretation ("This can be explained by…", "may"), and the limitations sections state concrete scope limits. Citation density is high in introductions (3–8 author-year citations per paragraph) and low in results paragraphs.

Passages below are copied verbatim from the PDF text (dehyphenated). They are exemplars of style only.

---

## Semantic Evaluation for Text-to-SQL with Distilled Test Suites (Zhong, Yu, Klein), 2020, https://arxiv.org/abs/2010.02840

**Abstract.** We propose test suite accuracy to approximate semantic accuracy for Text-to-SQL models. Our method distills a small test suite of databases that achieves high code coverage for the gold query from a large number of randomly generated databases. At evaluation time, it computes the denotation accuracy of the predicted queries on the distilled test suite, hence calculating a tight upper-bound for semantic accuracy efficiently. We use our proposed method to evaluate 21 models submitted to the Spider leader board and manually verify that our method is always correct on 100 examples. In contrast, the current Spider metric leads to a 2.5% false negative rate on average and 8.1% in the worst case, indicating that test suite accuracy is needed. Our implementation, along with distilled test suites for eleven Text-to-SQL datasets, is publicly available.

**Introduction, first paragraph.** A Text-to-SQL model translates natural language instructions to SQL queries that can be executed on databases and bridges the gap between expert programmers and non-experts. Accordingly, researchers have built a diversity of datasets (Dahl, 1989; Iyer et al., 2017; Zhong et al., 2017; Yu et al., 2018) and improved model performances (Xu et al., 2017; Suhr et al., 2018; Guo et al., 2019; Bogin et al., 2019a; Wang et al., 2020). However, evaluating the semantic accuracy of a Text-to-SQL model is a long-standing problem: we want to know whether the predicted SQL query has the same denotation as the gold for every possible database. "Single" denotation evaluation executes the predicted SQL query on one database and compares its denotation with that of the gold. It might create false positives, where a semantically different …

**Results paragraph.** For each gold query in Spider, we generate on average 94 neighbor queries (Figure 2). We sample 1000 random databases for each database schema and run fuzzing (Section 4.2) to construct S_g, which takes around a week on 16 CPUs. Figure 5 plots the fraction of neighbor queries that remain undistinguished after attempting t random databases. Checking single database denotation fails to distinguish 5% of the neighbor queries, and the curve stops decreasing after around 600 random databases; 1000 random databases can distinguish > 99% of the neighbor queries. A large number of random databases is necessary to achieve comprehensive code coverage.

**Discussion paragraph.** Our framework for creating test suites is general and only has two requirements: (1) the input is strongly typed so that the fuzzing distribution I_g can be defined and the sample input can be meaningfully executed, and (2) there exist neighbor queries N_g that are semantically close but different from the gold g. Since these two conditions hold in many execution environments, our framework might potentially be applied to other logical forms, such as λ-DCS …

---

## Logic-LM: Empowering Large Language Models with Symbolic Solvers for Faithful Logical Reasoning (Pan, Albalak, Wang, Wang), 2023, https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.12295

**Abstract.** Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown human-like reasoning abilities but still struggle with complex logical problems. This paper introduces a novel framework, LOGIC-LM, which integrates LLMs with symbolic solvers to improve logical problem-solving. Our method first utilizes LLMs to translate a natural language problem into a symbolic formulation. Afterward, a deterministic symbolic solver performs inference on the formulated problem. We also introduce a self-refinement module, which utilizes the symbolic solver's error messages to revise symbolic formalizations. We demonstrate LOGIC-LM's effectiveness on five logical reasoning datasets: ProofWriter, PrOntoQA, FOLIO, LogicalDeduction, and AR-LSAT. On average, LOGIC-LM achieves a significant performance boost of 39.2% over using LLM alone with standard prompting and 18.4% over LLM with chain-of-thought prompting. Our findings suggest that LOGIC-LM, by combining LLMs with symbolic logic, offers a promising avenue for faithful logical reasoning.

**Introduction, first paragraph.** Logical reasoning is a cognitive process that involves using evidence, arguments, and logic to arrive at conclusions or make judgments (Huang and Chang, 2023). It plays a central role in intelligent systems for problem-solving, decision-making, and critical thinking. Recently, large language models (LLMs) (Brown et al., 2020; Ouyang et al., 2022a; OpenAI, 2023) have exhibited emergent ability to "reason" like human (Wei et al., 2022a). When prompted with step-wise explanations of reasoning ("chain of thoughts"), or a simple prompt "Let's think step by step.", these models are able to answer questions with explicit reasoning steps (Wei et al., 2022b; Kojima et al., 2022).

**Results paragraph.** We report the results of LOGIC-LM (without self-refinement) and baselines in Table 2. For LOGIC-LM, a symbolic solver does not return an answer when there are grammar errors in the symbolic formulation. For these un-executable cases, we fall back on using chain-of-thought to predict the answer. We have three major observations. 1. Logic-LM significantly outperforms standard LLMs and CoT across all datasets. With GPT-3.5, our method outperforms standard LLM on all datasets, with an average improvement of 39.2%. This highlights the benefit of combining LLMs with external symbolic solvers for logical reasoning.

**Limitations paragraph.** We identify two main limitations of LOGIC-LM. First, LOGIC-LM relies on translating reasoning problems into logical formats that can be tackled by symbolic solvers. As a consequence, the model's applicability is inherently bounded by the expressiveness of the symbolic solver, for example, not all problems can be easily encoded in first-order logic. Nevertheless, this limitation can be mitigated by integrating a more diverse set of symbolic solvers.

---

## LINC: A Neurosymbolic Approach for Logical Reasoning by Combining Language Models with First-Order Logic Provers (Olausson, Gu, Lipkin, Zhang, Solar-Lezama, Tenenbaum, Levy), 2023, https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.15164

**Abstract.** Logical reasoning, i.e., deductively inferring the truth value of a conclusion from a set of premises, is an important task for artificial intelligence with wide potential impacts on science, mathematics, and society. While many prompting-based strategies have been proposed to enable Large Language Models (LLMs) to do such reasoning more effectively, they still appear unsatisfactory, often failing in subtle and unpredictable ways. In this work, we investigate the validity of instead reformulating such tasks as modular neurosymbolic programming, which we call LINC: Logical Inference via Neurosymbolic Computation. In LINC, the LLM acts as a semantic parser, translating premises and conclusions from natural language to expressions in first-order logic. These expressions are then offloaded to an external theorem prover, which symbolically performs deductive inference. Leveraging this approach, we observe significant performance gains on FOLIO and a balanced subset of ProofWriter for three different models in nearly all experimental conditions we evaluate. …

**Introduction, first paragraph.** Widespread adoption of large language models (LLMs) such as GPT-3 (Brown et al., 2020), GPT-4 (OpenAI, 2023), and PaLM (Chowdhery et al., 2022) have led to a series of remarkable successes in tasks ranging from text summarization to program synthesis. Some of these successes have encouraged the hypothesis that such models are able to flexibly and systematically reason (Huang and Chang, 2022), especially when using prompting strategies that explicitly encourage verbalizing intermediate reasoning steps before generating the final answer (Nye et al., 2021; Wei et al., 2022; Kojima et al., 2022; Wang et al., 2023b). However, this reasoning ability appears to be unreliable for tasks that require reasoning out of domain (Liang et al., 2022; Saparov et al., 2023), understanding negation (Anil et al., 2022), and following long reasoning chains (Dziri et al., 2023).

**Results paragraph.** Our main results are shown in Figure 3. Each bar represents either LINC or one of the three baselines, while each group of bars indicates the language model used (in {StarCoder+, GPT-3.5, GPT-4}). We note first that in the FOLIO domain (Figure 3a), StarCoder+—the smallest model we experiment with—benefits the most from LINC, achieving a mean accuracy that is 14.2 points higher than the closest controlled baseline (56.0% vs. 41.8% with CoT). We find that Scratchpad, where the intermediate logical formulae are still generated but the call to the symbolic solver is ablated and replaced by the model's own prediction, does not appear to benefit performance.

**Limitations paragraph.** Narrow scope of logical reasoning task considered: In this work, we focus exclusively on one aspect of logical reasoning: predicting the truth value of a conclusion given a set of natural language premises. Here, we consider a setting where the premises and conclusion are expressed in relatively short statements, which makes the formalization task tractable. In particular, ProofWriter's natural language statements are synthetically generated, so they can be easily and accurately parsed into FOL. FOLIO reflects a more naturalistic dataset, so we see a higher failure rate in LINC's semantic parsing step.

---

## SyGNS: A Systematic Generalization Testbed Based on Natural Language Semantics (Yanaka, Mineshima, Bekki, Inui), 2021, https://arxiv.org/abs/2106.01077

**Abstract.** Recently, deep neural networks (DNNs) have achieved great success in semantically challenging NLP tasks, yet it remains unclear whether DNN models can capture compositional meanings, those aspects of meaning that have been long studied in formal semantics. To investigate this issue, we propose a Systematic Generalization testbed based on Natural language Semantics (SyGNS), whose challenge is to map natural language sentences to multiple forms of scoped meaning representations, designed to account for various semantic phenomena. Using SyGNS, we test whether neural networks can systematically parse sentences involving novel combinations of logical expressions such as quantifiers and negation. Experiments show that Transformer and GRU models can generalize to unseen combinations of quantifiers, negations, and modifiers that are similar to given training instances in form, but not to the others. …

**Introduction, first paragraph.** Deep neural networks (DNNs) have shown impressive performance in various language understanding tasks (Wang et al., 2019a,b, i.a.), including semantically challenging tasks such as Natural Language Inference (NLI; Dagan et al., 2013; Bowman et al., 2015). However, a number of studies to probe DNN models with various NLI datasets (Naik et al., 2018; Dasgupta et al., 2018; Yanaka et al., 2019; Kim et al., 2019; Richardson et al., 2020; Saha et al., 2020; Geiger et al., 2020) have reported that current DNN models have some limitations to generalize to diverse semantic phenomena …

**Results paragraph.** Table 4 shows the accuracy by quantifier type. When the existential quantifier one was the primitive quantifier, the accuracy on the problems involving existential quantifiers, which have the same type as the primitive quantifier, was nearly perfect. Similarly, when the universal quantifier every was the primitive quantifier, the accuracy on the problems involving universal quantifiers was much better than that on the problems involving other quantifier types. These results indicate that models can easily generalize to problems involving quantifiers of the same type as the primitive quantifier, while the models struggle with generalizing to the others.

**Conclusion paragraph.** We have introduced an analysis method using SyGNS, a testbed for diagnosing the systematic generalization capability of DNN models in semantic parsing. We found that GRU and Transformer generalized to unseen combinations of semantic phenomena whose meaning representations are similar in forms to those in a training set, while the models struggle with generalizing to the others. In addition, these models failed to generalize to cases involving nested clauses.

---

## Assessing the Sensitivity and Alignment of FOL Closeness Metrics (Thatikonda, Buntine, Shareghi), 2025, https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.08613

**Abstract.** The recent successful paradigm of solving logical reasoning problems with tool-augmented large language models (LLMs) leverages translation of natural language (NL) statements into First-Order Logic (FOL) and external theorem provers. However, the correctness of FOL statements, comprising operators and text, often go unverified due to the lack of a reliable evaluation metric for comparing generated and ground-truth FOLs. In this paper, we conduct a comprehensive study on the sensitivity of existing NL-, FOL-, and graph-based metrics to capture differences between a sampled FOL and its corresponding ground-truth. We then measure the alignment between a metric-based ranking of FOL outputs and a strong LLM as-a-judge. To do this, we first apply operator and text-based perturbations to ground-truth FOL statements to assess metric sensitivity. We then evaluate metric robustness by comparing the metrics against LLMs judgment. Our empirical findings highlight a clear oversensitivity in the n-gram metric BLEU for text perturbations. …

**Introduction, first paragraph.** Large language models (LLMs) have advanced natural language reasoning, but logical and mathematical reasoning have long relied on formal, structured languages for proving deductions and theorems, a process that predates deep neural networks (Candela et al., 2006). This approach remains relevant today, especially for reasoning tasks that can be solved using formal statements. In case of first-order logic (FOL), LLM generations are used as intermediate steps and subsequently passed to theorem provers to solve the problem (Pan et al., 2023; Ye et al., 2023; Olausson et al., 2023).

**Results paragraph.** We present results from the two variations. Perturbation Analysis. Table 2 shows the average score by metrics for variations of perturbations. Here, we observe that BLEU score identifies text perturbations more effectively than specialized FOL and graph metrics. LE and Smatch++ exhibit sensitivity to all the perturbations …

**Limitations paragraph.** We recognize that GPT models used in our experiments are continually evolving, which may lead to variations in results over time. To manage the computational cost of generating multiple samples, we limited the data sample used in the experiments to 3 samples. This work could ideally be extended to a larger dataset or used as a reference for achieving high performance in existing methodologies, but not as a standalone solution. The FOLIO dataset, despite being widely used, may contain errors inherent to human judgement.

---

## Section outlines

**Semantic Evaluation for Text-to-SQL with Distilled Test Suites**
1 Introduction · 2 Problem Statement · 3 Desiderata (3.1 Measure Coverage through Neighbors; 3.2 Optimization Objective) · 4 Fuzzing (4.1 Sampling Databases; 4.2 Distilling a Test Suite) · 5 Evaluation Setup (5.1 Dataset; 5.2 Model-Predicted Queries; 5.3 Metric Adaptation) · 6 Results (6.1 Reliability; 6.2 Errors of Traditional Metrics; 6.3 Correlation with Existing Metrics; 6.4 Computational Efficiency) · 7 Metrics Comparison and Analysis · 8 Discussion and Conclusion
Method organised by pipeline stage: objective, then sampling, then distillation.
Results organised by question: is the metric reliable, how wrong are the old metrics, how do they correlate, what does it cost.

1 Introduction · 2 Related Work · 3 LOGIC-LM (3.1 Problem Formulator; 3.2 Symbolic Reasoner; 3.3 Self-Refiner; 3.4 Result Interpreter) · 4 Experiments (4.1 Main Results; 4.2 Effectiveness of Problem Formulator; 4.3 Robustness of Reasoning; 4.4 Impact of Self-Refinement; 4.5 Case Study) · 5 Conclusion and Future Work · Limitations

Method organised by evaluation concept (systematicity, productivity), then representations and data.
